1. **Routes:** `POST /api/v1/connect/consent-records/:id/withdraw` (`withdrawn-via: api`) and the console action (`withdrawn-via: operator`), with an optional `reason` from a closed list (`subject-request`, `operator-decision`, `other`); a second withdrawal is 409 `consent_already_withdrawn`.
2. **Effects, in one transaction:** the record is withdrawn; its token, if `created`, becomes `expired` with `expiry-reason: consent_withdrawn`; if `pending`, its job fails at commit with `consent_withdrawn` (the record is locked `FOR UPDATE`, the job holds it `FOR SHARE`); if `redeemed`, nothing is recalled, and `connect/share-token.consent-withdrawn` goes to the counterparty's environment so its operator can act (ours). `consent-record.withdrawn` is emitted in the record's own environment.
3. **No swap of basis** ([L2] ¶121-123): a withdrawn record can be cited by nothing; no route changes a record's method, purpose, recipient or categories.
4. **The end user's channel** (decision 28): withdrawal is operator-mediated in this release once the flow has ended (OpenVerify has no end-user account), with the flow exit of C237.6 while the session lives; the built-in share-consent text tells the person how to withdraw through the organisation's own contact, filled into `{withdraw}` from the step's required `withdrawal-contact` (plain text, 1–500 characters; publish refuses a `share-consent` step without it, `share_consent_withdrawal_contact_missing`; review H06), with the version's `privacy-url` link, when set, in Phase 9's fixed link slot (C162.3's link fields); a test asserts that `{withdraw}` never renders empty. Whether operator-mediated withdrawal is "as easy as" consent under Art. 7(3) is a question for counsel, listed under Q7 (review P12). A self-service withdrawal link is deferred (C286).
5. **Honesty:** withdrawal stops every future platform transfer under the record; a copy already made belongs to its controller, and the docs say both operators must act on the event.
6. **Withdrawal in the flow and its effects** (review P12): (1) while the flow session lives, every screen after an accepted `share-consent` offers a withdrawal exit, `POST /api/v1/flow/share-consents/:step-id/withdraw` (`withdrawn-via: flow`, the same effects as 2). Where it is offered (verification V02): the session response's `withdrawable-share-consents[] {step-id, side, recipient-name}` lists this session's accepted, unwithdrawn `share-consent` records, and both clients render one "withdraw" affordance per entry on every later step and on the completion screen. The route accepts only a `step-id` listed there (else 409 `flow_step_not_current`, Phase 9's code), acts on that passed step alone, and never moves the position: the one exception to Phase 9 C151.4's current-step rule (Locked contracts). `contracts-11a` (K) freezes its schema, the member and their golden fixtures; (2) withdrawing a destination-side record after redemption sets `provenance.consent-withdrawn-at` on the destination's copies, which then stop counting under `accept-cloned-results` (C235.4) and show the withdrawal beside their Cloned marker; (3) `consent-record.withdrawn` and `connect/share-token.consent-withdrawn` join Phase 8's triggers beside `inbound-created` (C243.2), so either side can automate its response.

### C238. The `share-consent` step and client-side redemption (connect R17, R24, R26; 1.8; Phase 9 C175.2; Phase 10 C194.1)

1. **The step** (flow-spec v2, a new member of the step union): `{type: 'share-consent', id, side: 'source' | 'destination', connection-id (required for the source side; the destination side takes the inquiry's token), consent-valid-seconds (60–1,814,400, default 1,209,600), withdrawal-contact (C237.4), copy-keys?}`. Declining never ends the flow (ours; consent must be free, [L2] ¶13): on the source side the flow continues and a `declined` record is made (C236.11); on the destination side the token stays `created` but can no longer be redeemed through this inquiry or by API (C234.1), and the flow continues as a fresh verification.
2. **Publish checks** (Phase 9 C154.9 style, `definition_invalid` codes): a source-side step's connection must be an outbound connection of the template's organisation (`share_consent_connection_invalid`; a `pending` connection publishes with the warning `share_consent_connection_not_active`); a `share-consent` step may not follow a completion (`share_consent_after_completion`); a destination-side step must precede every verification step that `accept-cloned-results` lists (`share_consent_after_cloned_step`). At run time the engine skips a destination-side step on an inquiry created without a share token. **One publish-check hook** (verification V06): `foundation-11a` places a single call in Phase 9's publish file to `services/connect/publish-checks.ts` (`api-consent`, with the pure rules in `connect-logic`'s checkpoint 4). The hook runs every Connect publish check of this phase: the three above with their warning, `share_consent_connect_disabled` (C231.10), `cloned_results_without_binding` (C235.8), `share_consent_withdrawal_contact_missing` (C237.4) and `share_consent_retention_missing` (C238.9). It records the acknowledgements `cloned-results` (C235.3), `cloned-results-unbound` (C235.8) and `share-consent-default-text` (C238.10) through Phase 9's existing publish-acknowledgement path. There is one test per code and per acknowledgement.
3. **The client view** (server-composed; ours): `{type, id, side, recipient-name, source-name, categories[], text {locale, body, sha256, keys[]}, biometric? {body, sha256}}`. The server builds `body` from the built-in keys `share.consent.*` (each `legal: consent` in Phase 9's `COPY_KEYS`) or the version's operator copy for them, fills the closed placeholders `{source}`, `{recipient}`, `{categories}`, `{expires}`, `{withdraw}` and, on the destination side, `{retention}` (C238.9) itself, normalises to NFC and digests the logical text; the client composes nothing. No token id, connection id or source object id is in the client view.
4. **Exits** (flow API, flow session, current-step rule of Phase 9 C151.12): `POST /api/v1/flow/share-consents/:step-id/accept` with `{text-sha256, biometric-text-sha256?, explicit-biometric}` and `POST …/decline` with `{}`. The server compares the digests with what it served at this position (422 `consent_text_mismatch`), requires the biometric tick when the share carries `biometric` (422 `explicit_consent_required`), and creates the `flow` consent record (C236.4). On the destination side the accept also enqueues the redemption into this inquiry in C234.11's `fill-inquiry` mode (ours, labelled: the vendor's client-side redemption fills an inquiry and completes it when the data covers the template, connect 1.8): fields are hydrated, and every shareable verification is a clone with its provenance and marker, counted only under C235.4; the flow shows Phase 9's waiting position until the job ends, then the engine skips the steps that C235.4 counts as satisfied and asks for the rest.
5. **No token in any URL** (connect R17, 3.2; ASVS v5.0.0-14.2.1; RFC 9700 §4.3): client-side redemption starts only from an inquiry the destination's backend creates with `POST /api/v1/inquiries` and `meta.share-token-id` (scopes `inquiries:write` and `connect-share-tokens:redeem`); the token must be inbound to the caller's environment, `created` and unexpired, and the template must contain a destination-side `share-consent` step (422 `share_consent_step_missing`); one inquiry per token (409 `share_token_in_use`, recorded in `reserved_inquiry_id`). The person reaches that inquiry through today's session or one-time-link credentials in the URL fragment. There is no template-id entry and no `share-token` query parameter anywhere.
6. **The web flow** (`apps/flow/src/steps/share-consent/`): the text, the recipient and categories in words, the separate unticked biometric tick when served, accept and decline, focus and announcements per Phase 9 C171; the built-in `share.consent.*` texts with `en` as the source, written to carry exactly C238.3's required content (who discloses, the named recipient, the categories, the purpose, the expiry, the destination's retention term and how to withdraw) and **pending counsel's review (Q7)**, marked so to the operator (C238.10; reviews P14, H08), and `es`, `fr`, `de` and `ar` machine-drafted and marked, under Phase 9's `locale-unreviewed:` rule for legal keys.
7. **Android** (`commonMain`, iOS-ready; Phase 10 C194.1): a `share-consent` renderer in `StepRegistry` (`ui/steps/ShareConsentStep.kt`, `flow/ShareConsent.kt`), `FlowApi.acceptShareConsent`, `declineShareConsent` and `withdrawShareConsent`, with the withdrawal affordance of C237.6 rendered from `withdrawable-share-consents` on every later step and the completion screen, as on the web (verification V02); before it echoes a digest, the SDK recomputes SHA-256 over the logical text it displays with its own `Sha256` (Phase 10 C200.2) and renders the unsupported screen on a mismatch, so it never reports a text it did not show; the members join `flow-spec-members.json`; the strings come from the web catalogs through the key map (Phase 10 C199.1); `Request` and `OneTimeLink` gain nothing (Phase 10 C223: the token never reaches a client).
8. **Honesty:** the digest records which text the server served and that the client reported showing it; it does not prove that the person read it.
9. **The retention term** (review P13; BIPA 740 ILCS 14/15(b) asks for the purpose and the length of term before biometric data is collected): `{retention}` is filled from the destination template's retention setting, else the environment's (C16), in words; publish refuses a destination-side step whose template has neither while the share can carry `biometric` (`share_consent_retention_missing`), and the destination text and the biometric tick both carry it.
10. **Counsel's review is pending** (reviews P14, H08): until Q7 records counsel's review, the `en` `share.consent.*` keys carry the review status `counsel-unreviewed` beside Phase 9's locale review status, the template editor shows the built-in `legal: consent` texts as "not reviewed by counsel", and publishing a version whose `share-consent` step uses them needs the audited acknowledgement `share-consent-default-text` (Phase 9's publish acknowledgements); `docs/connect/consent.md` and the release notes (C284.4) say so, and the release checklist's Q7 line covers these texts.

### C239. Events and webhooks (connect R18; 3.3)

1. **Names:**
   - the vendor's four: `connect/share-token.outbound-created` and `connect/share-token.outbound-redeemed` (source environment), `connect/share-token.inbound-created` and `connect/share-token.inbound-redeemed` (destination environment); `connect/share-token.ready` is not emitted (no prefetch, decision 21);
   - ours: `connect/share-token.expired` and `connect/share-token.failed` (both sides), `connect/share-token.consent-withdrawn` (the counterparty of a redeemed share), `connect/share-token.source-redacted` (the destination of a redeemed share whose source was redacted later; advisory only), `connect/connection.created`, `.activated`, `.deactivated`, `.reactivated` and `.share-policy-changed` (every environment of both organisations), `consent-record.created`, `consent-record.declined` (C236.11) and `consent-record.withdrawn` (the record's environment).
2. **Payloads:** the resource as the receiving side sees it (C232.3), never shared data; the webhook attribute blocklist, PII masking and the redaction stub apply.
3. **Samples and words:** every name has a sample payload, a dashboard group (`Connect`) and its words in the console, as earlier event families.
4. **`eventSubject`** (connect 3.3): `connect/share-token` and `connect/connection` heads map to their own subjects beside `report/`, by a rule over the `connect/` prefix with a test over every `EVENT_NAMES` entry; `consent-record` is flat.
5. **Honesty:** `inbound-created` tells the destination that a share exists; it carries no shared data, and the data moves only on redemption.

### C240. Scopes, authorization, limits and audit (connect R16, R19; 3.2)

1. **Scopes** (connect R19, renamed to OpenVerify's style; decision 35): `connect-connections:read`, `connect-connections:write`, `connect-share-tokens:read`, `connect-share-tokens:write` (create, expire, withdrawal), `connect-share-tokens:redeem`, `connect-share-tokens:peek`, and ours `connect-consent-records:attest` (C236.2, review P03); the operator permission `connect-connections:activate`. Built-in roles: `admin` holds all; the read-only roles get the two `:read` scopes; `peek`, `attest` and `activate` join `NOT_FOR_VIEWERS`; `connect-connections:write` and `:activate` are organisation-level (C231.9); `PERMISSION_INFO` labels each; a peek by a user also needs `pii:view`.
2. **The authorization matrix** (a contract test and a Testcontainers suite): every Connect route against each caller (the source organisation's environment of the token's kind and of the other kind, the destination's of each kind, a third organisation, a self-connection's single organisation) and each principal (an API key with and without the scope, a sandbox and a production key, a user with and without `pii:view`, with an environment-scoped and an organisation-wide assignment, with and without `environments:production`), with the expected status; every refusal of an unseen object is the one 404 of C232.8.
3. **Rate policies** (C12 amended; provisional, ours, with the basis that a destination redeems about one share per onboarding): `connect-redeem` 60 a minute and `connect-peek` 60 a minute, beside the existing `api` policy. Each keeps **one bucket per principal and environment pair** (verification V11; C12's limiter keys one bucket per policy and caller key, `apps/api/src/rate-limit/index.ts:98-102`): an API key belongs to one environment, so it has one bucket, and a user session has one bucket for each environment it acts in; `GET /api/v1/rate-limits` lists them.
4. **Audit** (connect 3.2): append-only entries, in the environment where the act happened and in the counterparty's: connection created, activated, deactivated, reactivated, policy changed; token created, peeked, redeemed, expired, failed; consent recorded, declined, withdrawn. In the actor's own environment each entry names its actor (user, API key, workflow or the system); in the counterparty's it names only `{organization-id, kind}` (review P26: the other controller's staff are not disclosed); never data.
5. **One cross-tenant path** (ours; reviews P24, F22): every read **and write** of another environment's rows goes through `apps/api/src/services/connect/cross-tenant.ts`, which takes a token or connection already bound to the caller and refuses anything else: the reads of peek and redemption, the counterparty's staged events (C239.1), its audit entries (C240.4), a deactivation expiring the source's tokens (C231.4) and a withdrawal expiring the other side's token (C237.2). It also covers the access export's read of Connect tokens and consent records on the counterparty's side (C253.2), as the entry point `listSharesTouching(environment, object ids)`, which `api-connect` builds in 11A so that `dsar-export` only calls it. The guard is an import boundary, not a data-flow guess (verification V04):
   - **Two modules per repository.** Each Connect repository is split in two. Its own-environment functions (create, list and read within the caller's environment, as every repository does) are imported by the Connect services like any repository. Its cross-environment functions, which take the counterparty's environment id, live in a separate `*.cross-env.ts` module.
   - **The rule.** An ESLint `no-restricted-imports` rule and a source test allow the `*.cross-env.ts` modules, and the event and audit writers' counterparty entry points, to be imported only by `cross-tenant.ts`.
   - **Tests.** A Testcontainers test covers each cross-environment write, plus the export's read.
6. **Honesty:** scopes and limits bound what a key can do on the platform; they cannot stop a recipient from misusing data it was entitled to receive.
7. **Cross-tenant probes over the whole API** (review P24): a generated Testcontainers test walks every path of `docs/api/openapi.json` with an id parameter and calls it with organisation B's key and session on organisation A's ids (seeded per resource family); each answers the identical generic 404 body. It is the ASVS v5.0.0-8.4.1 row's evidence beyond Connect, owned by `integration-11a`. `integration-11b` owns it in 11B and extends it with 11B's families and their seeds: SSO providers, end-sessions and account exports (`aexp_`). No later Workflow adds a route with an id parameter; if one does, its integration agent takes the file the same way (verification V14).

### C241. Redaction and retention of shares (connect 3.2, 2.6; C16)

1. **A source redacted before redemption:** in the redaction transaction, every `created` token whose source is the redacted object (or an object inside it) becomes `failed` with `source_redacted`, with its events; a `pending` job fails at commit with the same reason.
2. **A source redacted after redemption:** nothing changes at the destination (its copy is its own); the destination receives `connect/share-token.source-redacted`, advisory only, and the docs say that its operator decides what its own law requires.
3. **Consent records** (decision 29): a record is redacted with the data it covers (the subject's redaction clears its sealed evidence, its text keys and its link to the subject); its bare fact (time, side, purpose, recipient, categories, text digest, method, withdrawal) is kept for the environment's `consent-proof-retention-days` (0–3,650, default 0) after the covered data is gone, then deleted ([L2] ¶107). A `consent_texts` row is deleted by the retention pass when no record cites its digest; an attested text goes with its record's redaction (C230.5); a destination record is redacted with the copy it covers (C236.10).
4. **Tokens:** `expired` and `failed` rows are deleted 30 days after they ended, as one-time links are (C16). A `redeemed` row is the disclosure record (review P04; decision 36): it holds ids only (no shared value), and it is kept while the source object or any destination copy exists, so a later source redaction or withdrawal still reaches the destination (`connect/share-token.source-redacted`, `.consent-withdrawn`; GDPR Art. 19) and a source-side access export still names the recipient (Art. 15(1)(c), C253.2); the retention pass deletes it once both sides are redacted or gone. Provenance keeps the token id as a plain string. A Testcontainers test redacts the source 31 days after a redemption and sees the destination's event.
5. **Connections and policies:** kept; their sealed `agreement-reference` is never redacted by an object's redaction (it holds no data subject's data, by the field's own help text).
6. **Destination copies:** redacted like any object; a cloned file's objects are deleted with every version (the existing storage rule).
7. **Honesty:** the platform cannot reach a copy outside itself, and backups keep redacted data until they expire (C277.6).

### C242. The console (connect R3, R20, R22; Phase 9 shell)

1. **Settings → Connect:** connections in both directions with status, counterparty and policy version; a connection page with the policy in words and a diff between versions, the agreement reference (destination side, `pii:view` not needed: it is not personal data), and the actions each side may take (activate with the step-up of C231.3 and the policy shown before the confirmation; deactivate; reactivate; change the policy).
2. **Share tokens:** inbound and outbound lists with filters; a token page with its status history, `redeem-summary`, the peek view (destination, `connect-share-tokens:peek` and `pii:view`, each attribute shown as its value or "present" / "not present"), and redeem (mode, destination, mappings, account linking) and expire forms.
3. **Consent records** on the inquiry, account and transaction pages: side, method ("witnessed in the flow" or "attested by the operator, not witnessed"), recipient, categories, the stored text by digest, expiry, and withdraw with a confirmation.
4. **Provenance:** a "Cloned" marker on every cloned verification and check and a banner on a cloned inquiry naming the token and the source organisation (the vendor's marker, connect R20); hydrated fields listed with their token on the object's page; "relied on a cloned result" on a decision that did.
5. **Tests:** RSC payload tests (no peek value, consent evidence or agreement reference reaches a page without the permission it needs), component tests per state, the Phase 9 accessibility rules (C171) with axe in tests only.
6. **Honesty:** the console never shows a cloned result without its marker.

### C243. Workflow actions and the triggers (connect R22; Phase 8 C122, C118)

1. **Actions** (Phase 8 C122): `create_share_token` (`connection-id`, `source-id`, `consent-record-id`, `expires-in-seconds?`), `redeem_share_token` (`share-token-id`, the redeem body of C234.1), `expire_share_token` (`share-token-id`); each with a zod input, a typed output in the run scope (the token resource), idempotent by run step (Phase 8's effect key), the workflow as the audit actor.
2. **Triggers:** `connect/share-token.inbound-created`, so a destination can redeem on arrival (the vendor's pattern); `consent-record.withdrawn` and `connect/share-token.consent-withdrawn`, so either side can act on a withdrawal (C237.6, review P12).
3. **Not actions** (ours): activating a connection, creating or withdrawing a consent record; each is a human's or the operator backend's act, and attesting needs its own scope (C236.2).
4. **Honesty:** an automatic redemption still needs a valid destination-side consent record.

### C244. Seeds, sandbox and fixtures for Connect

1. **`seed:dev --connect`** (ours): a second organisation, "Example Partner", with sandbox and production environments, an admin per built-in role, and a `pending` connection from the dev organisation to it with a sample policy; it prints nothing secret beyond what `seed:dev` already prints once. Self-connections are created by the e2e only.
2. **Golden fixtures** (`contracts/fixtures/api/connect/`): every resource as each side sees it, every problem, the source-data envelope per subtype, the share policy, the events' samples, the `share-consent` client view and its exits.
3. **Synthetic data only** (rule 3): names and emails at `example.com`, documentation IP ranges, synthetic documents and public-domain faces through the existing manifests.
4. **Honesty:** a self-connection tests the mechanism, not a partnership; the seeded partner organisation is labelled a sample in the console.

### C245. Console sessions: idle timeout and ending sessions (G-IDLE; ASVS v5.0.0-7.3.1, 7.4.2, 7.4.3, 7.4.5; Phase 9 C175.2)

1. **Idle timeout** (decision 9): a console session expires after 30 minutes without an authenticated request (`OPENVERIFY_CONSOLE_IDLE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`, 300–86,400, default 1,800) and after 12 hours in all (`USER_SESSION_TOKEN_TTL_SECONDS`, unchanged, now `OPENVERIFY_CONSOLE_ABSOLUTE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`, 3,600–86,400, default 43,200). `user_sessions.last_seen_at` slides on each request, written at most once a minute per session so a busy page does not write per request; the check runs where the session is resolved (`principal-resolver.ts`), before any permission.
2. **What a timeout looks like:** 401 `session_expired` with `reason: idle | absolute`; the console's BFF clears the cookie and shows the login page with "signed out after inactivity"; the timeout counts server requests only (a background poll from an open page does not count as activity: polling routes are marked `activity: false`).
3. **Sessions end** (V7.4.2, 7.4.3, 7.4.5; ours where Phase 9 left it): every session of a user is deleted when the user is disabled, loses its last role, has MFA reset (`user:reset-mfa`), changes or resets its password, or enrols the first factor of an organisation that requires MFA; an admin can end one user's sessions from the team page (`POST /api/v1/dashboard/users/:id/end-sessions`, `users:write`), and a user can end its other sessions. Each writes an audit entry. The inventory records which of these Phase 9 C168 already built; only the missing ones are built.
4. **Tests:** a frozen clock across both limits; the sliding write once a minute; each ending event; a Testcontainers race of a request against an idle expiry.
5. **Honesty:** an idle timeout limits the window of an unattended browser; it does not protect a session token stolen while in use.

### C246. The login limiter in Valkey (G-LOGIN; ASVS v5.0.0-6.3.x, 2.4.1; decision 10)

1. **Store** (review F19): the C4 windows (50 per address, 10 per email, two fixed 15-minute windows) move to Valkey as their own limiter, not a C12 GCRA policy: one Lua script checks and counts both keys atomically, keeping C4's rule that a refused attempt is counted against neither key; it is not in `RATE_LIMIT_POLICIES` and not listed by `GET /api/v1/rate-limits`. The `LoginRateLimiter` interface keeps its shape (it was written async for this move). The per-email key is an HMAC-SHA-256 of the normalised email under a dedicated subkey derived like the blind index's (label `openverify/login-limit/v1`), so no email sits in a Valkey key name, AOF file or `SCAN` output (review P32; a test that no key contains `@`).
2. **Fails closed for login only** (ours; H18 keeps the API limiter failing open): when Valkey is unreachable, login answers 503 `login_unavailable` with `Retry-After`, and the API's other routes keep failing open (except `connect-peek` and `account-export`, which fail closed, C233.5, C253.7), each with a warn log and the metric `rate_limiter_store_unavailable` (C250.7).
3. **Across organisations:** the per-email key is the normalised email across every organisation, as the login already checks every account with that email (C248.8).
4. **Tests:** two API instances share one budget (Testcontainers with two `buildApp`); the fail-closed path; the attempt-count rule unchanged ("counted against neither key when refused").
5. **Honesty:** limits slow online guessing; they do not stop a slow, distributed attempt, which the docs say MFA must cover.

### C247. API-key pepper rotation and scope bounds (B3-S1, B3-E1; ASVS v5.0.0-11.1, 8.2)

1. **Pepper rotation** (ours): `OPENVERIFY_API_KEY_PEPPER_PREVIOUS` (comma-separated, at most two) beside the current pepper. A key is looked up by its HMAC under the current pepper, then under each previous one; a key found under a previous pepper is re-hashed under the current one in the same request (a compare-and-set on the stored HMAC), so rotation completes as keys are used. `pnpm --filter @openverify/api api-keys:pepper-status` counts keys still under a previous pepper (never printing a key or a hash), so the operator removes the previous pepper only when the count is zero or the remaining keys may be invalidated. The self-hosting guide gives the procedure (C277.5).
2. **Display prefixes** stay plaintext, so a rotation changes nothing a customer sees.
3. **Scope bounds** (B3-E1, "to verify"): creating or cloning a key with a scope the creating principal does not hold is 403 `scope_exceeds_creator`; a user without `environments:production` cannot create a production key. The inventory records what Phase 2's C12 already enforces; the tests cover every route that mints a key.
4. **Honesty:** a pepper keeps a database-only leak from testing candidate keys; it does not help once the pepper itself leaks, and rotation then needs every key re-issued.

### C248. SSO: OIDC sign-in for the console (Phase 9 S15, C175.2 and its decision 48; ASVS v5.0.0-6.8, 10.x)

1. **Scope** (decision 37): OpenID Connect sign-in for console users, per organisation, with the authorization code flow, PKCE (`S256`) and `state` and `nonce`; one or more identity providers per organisation; tested with Okta-, Entra- and Google-shaped discovery documents served by an in-process provider in tests, never a real identity provider; the guide's SSO page names exactly those shapes as tested and says that real identity providers are untested and the operator's to verify (ADR 0002's tier A wording; review H16). SAML and SCIM move to the next release (C286).
2. **Library:** `openid-client` 6.8.8 (MIT, 2026-09-05), with its dependencies `oauth4webapi` 3.8.8 (MIT, 2026-09-05) and `jose` at a `^6.2.12` version that the release-age rule resolves (MIT; 6.2.12 is of 2026-09-05), each recorded in `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md` with its date by `contracts-11b`, which re-reads them on its day.
3. **Configuration** (`sso_providers (organization_id, id, issuer, client_id, client_secret sealed, allowed_email_domains text[], tenant_pin_claim, tenant_pin_value, required_amr text[], required_acr text[], jit_provisioning, default_role_keys text[], enforced, created_at, updated_at)`, the pin and `amr`/`acr` columns as below, verification V13; ours): managed in Settings → Security by `settings:write` held through an organisation-wide assignment (the provider settings are organisation-level in Phase 9's `SETTING_LEVEL`; review P01) with Phase 9's step-up; the client secret sealed with row AAD and in `ENCRYPTED_COLUMNS` and the rewrap list (review P35), never served; discovery and token calls through the SSRF-guarded outbound client (private ranges allowed outside production only, and in production only for issuers the install names in `OPENVERIFY_SSO_PRIVATE_ISSUERS`, an audited install setting no tenant can change; review P19). Per provider (review P07): `allowed_email_domains` is required and non-empty; the exact `iss` is stored, and a multi-tenant issuer (Microsoft's `common` or `organizations`, Google's shared issuer) is accepted only with a tenant pin (`tid` or `hd`) stored on the provider and checked on every ID token; `required_amr` or `required_acr` values (review P06).
4. **Sign-in:** the BFF routes `GET /api/v1/dashboard/sso/:organization-slug/start` and a callback per provider, `GET /api/v1/dashboard/sso/callback/:provider-id` (review P20: a per-provider redirect URI against mix-up, plus RFC 9207's `iss` response parameter checked where the provider advertises it), reached through the console's own route handlers (`apps/dashboard/src/app/(auth)/sso/[slug]/start/route.ts` and `…/sso/callback/[provider]/route.ts`; review F21), which hold the browser binding: `state`, `nonce`, the PKCE verifier and the provider id live in a single-use server-side record (10 minutes) keyed by a `__Host-` cookie the start handler sets; the request logger and the API logs record the callback without its query string (`sso-oidc`'s named borrowed edit to the request logger's serializer, with a log test; verification V13). The ID token is verified by the library (issuer, audience, `nonce`, expiry, algorithm allowlist RS256, ES256, PS256, EdDSA; no `none`; keys only from the issuer's JWKS, ASVS 9.1.2, 9.1.3). **Linking** (review P07): a returning user is matched only by its stored `(provider, issuer, subject)` in `user_sso_identities`; email matching is used only for the first link of an existing user, only inside `allowed_email_domains`, and only with `email_verified: true` present in the ID token (absent is false); once a link exists the email is never matched again; the first link of an existing user is audited and notified by mail when the mailer is configured. A new console session follows C245. Tests: a swapped provider, a replayed state, a missing cookie, a multi-tenant issuer without its pin, a missing `email_verified`, an email outside the domains.
5. **Just-in-time provisioning:** off by default; when on, a first sign-in with a verified email in `allowed_email_domains` creates a user with `default_role_keys`, with an audit entry. The bound is on permissions, not role keys (review P21): a just-in-time default role may hold no organisation-level permission and none of `settings:write`, `api-keys:*`, `connect-connections:activate`, `connect-consent-records:attest`, `accounts:export` or `environments:production`, checked when the provider is saved and again at each just-in-time sign-in against the role's current permissions (Phase 9's role editor can change them).
6. **Enforcement and second factors** (review P06): with `enforced`, password sign-in is refused for the organisation's users except the break-glass path (`user:allow-password <email>`, a `user:reset-mfa` style command granting one password sign-in within 24 hours, recorded in `users.password_login_allowed_until` and cleared on use, audited; review F11). With `require-mfa`, an SSO sign-in whose ID token does not meet the provider's `required_amr` or `required_acr` must pass the user's local factor before the session is created; the console shows the remote `amr` when the provider sends it. Step-up for an SSO session is an OIDC re-authentication (`prompt=login` with `max_age=0`, `auth_time` checked within Phase 9's 10-minute window and the same `amr`/`acr` values), which sets `mfa_verified_at`. Deprovisioning at the identity provider ends console access only at the next sign-in or the idle timeout (C245.1; SCIM is deferred, C286), a B4 row in the threat model.
7. **Problems:** `sso_not_configured`, `sso_state_invalid`, `sso_email_unverified`, `sso_user_unknown`, `sso_password_disabled`; all generic toward the browser, detailed only in the audit and the logs as codes.
8. **The organisation hint** (the Phase 1 carry "login across organisations"): C4 login takes an optional `organization` (slug) that narrows the accounts checked; without it the login still checks every account with that email in constant time per candidate, as today; SSO start always names the slug.
9. **Honesty:** OIDC moves authentication to the operator's identity provider; the console's own authorisation (roles, environments, `pii:view`) still decides what a user may do.

### C249. Data stores: TLS, secrets as files, the migration role and the migrate lock (G-TLS, G-SECRETS, B7-E1, B8-S1; ASVS v5.0.0-12.3.1, 12.3.3, 12.3.4, 13.3.1)

1. **TLS settings** (decision 13): `DATABASE_URL` honours `sslmode=verify-full` with `OPENVERIFY_POSTGRES_CA_FILE`; `REDIS_URL` accepts `rediss://` with `OPENVERIFY_REDIS_CA_FILE` and a username for Valkey ACL users; the S3 endpoint accepts `https` with `OPENVERIFY_S3_CA_FILE`; `OPENVERIFY_VISION_URL` accepts `https` with `OPENVERIFY_VISION_CA_FILE`; SMTP keeps STARTTLS. **In production mode**, the API, worker and migrate log one warn line per store whose transport an interceptor could read or replace at start (`insecure_transport` with the store's name and the reason, never the URL): plain text, a Postgres `sslmode` below `verify-full`, or TLS without a trusted CA (review P31); no setting turns certificate verification off. They do not refuse to start (in-cluster setups vary); the self-hosting guide requires TLS across hosts and states the accepted risk inside one namespace (C277.4).
2. **Secrets as files** (G-SECRETS; ours): every secret `config.ts` reads (the KEK and its previous keys, the blind-index key, the pepper and its previous peppers, the vision and flow-server tokens, the BFF token, provider secrets, SMTP and store credentials) also reads from `<NAME>_FILE`, a path to a mounted file (trailing newline trimmed); setting both is a start-up error; a world-readable file is a warning (review P18: group read is how a non-root process reads a Kubernetes secret mounted `0440` with `fsGroup`, so it is not warned). The variables come from the registry's patterns too (C277.2), so every provider secret has its `_FILE` form. A KMS adapter for the KEK is deferred (C286; PLAN §3 corrected, C282).
3. **Two database roles** (decision 14; B7-E1): `OPENVERIFY_MIGRATION_DATABASE_URL` for `db:migrate`, `seed:dev` and the operator commands that change schema; the runtime processes use `DATABASE_URL`. `pnpm --filter @openverify/api db:grant-runtime <role>` (run with the migration role) grants the runtime role `SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE` on every table and sequence except the append-only ones, `USAGE` on the schema, and default privileges for future tables, never `CREATE`. **Append-only tables** (review P17): `audit_logs` and `connect_share_policies` get `SELECT, INSERT` only, and a `BEFORE DELETE` trigger refuses every delete except two (verification V05):
   - one made while `current_user` is the migration role, which owns the table and the function below, and so is the effective user inside that `SECURITY DEFINER` call;
   - one coming from the environment cascade (`pg_trigger_depth() > 1`, a delete fired by the foreign key's cascade).

   There is no session setting to bypass it (unlike the UPDATE trigger's `openverify.kek_rewrap`, which any session could set and which would be no control for deletes); Phase 9's `audit-log-retention-days` (C168.6) is enforced through `openverify_delete_expired_audit_logs(environment_id)`, a `SECURITY DEFINER` function owned by the migration role that deletes only rows older than the environment's setting (never less than its 30-day minimum) with their `audit_log_ip_index` rows, the only path the runtime has; `kek:rewrap`, which rewrites audit ciphertexts, runs under the migration role. **Maintenance statements** (review F02): `contracts-11b`'s inventory lists every statement the runtime runs that needs ownership (today `ANALYZE` in `watchlist-key-df.ts:30` and `watchlists.ts:539`, and `gin_clean_pending_list` at `watchlist-key-df.ts:60-61`; and every `VACUUM`, `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW`, `TRUNCATE`, `LOCK` or `REINDEX` Phases 6–10 add); `db:grant-runtime` grants PostgreSQL 17's `MAINTAIN` on exactly those tables, and the GIN cleanup moves into `openverify_gin_clean_pending_lists(source_id)`, a `SECURITY DEFINER` function owned by the migration role and created in the 11B migration (a named borrowed edit to `watchlist-key-df.ts` by `platform-ops`). Without the variable, `db:migrate` uses `DATABASE_URL` and logs a production warning. Testcontainers tests prove the runtime role can neither `CREATE TABLE`, `DROP` a table, disable the audit trigger nor `DELETE FROM audit_logs` directly (with or without any `SET LOCAL` first), that `openverify_delete_expired_audit_logs` deletes the rows past the environment's setting and keeps those within it (V05), and that a list sync, the key-frequency rebuild and a re-key complete under it with no `WARNING`; the integration suite's sync tests run as a non-owner role.
4. **The migrate lock** (hardening 7.2): `db:migrate` takes a session advisory lock (`pg_advisory_lock` on a fixed key derived from `openverify:migrate`) around the migrations and the event-id backfill, waits at most `OPENVERIFY_MIGRATE_LOCK_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` (default 600) and then exits 75 with `migrate_lock_timeout`; a second concurrent run waits and then finds nothing to do. A Testcontainers test runs two at once. The migration creates `vector` itself (`CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS vector`, the convention), and the guide gives the managed-Postgres step for a provider that needs its admin role.
5. **Valkey** (B8-S1, B8-D1): the API, worker and migrate accept a per-process Valkey user; the worker warns at start when `maxmemory-policy` is not `noeviction` (Phase 8's check, kept); the guide gives the ACL lines that deny `FLUSHALL`, `FLUSHDB`, `CONFIG`, `DEBUG` and `KEYS` to application users.
6. **Honesty:** the warnings and the role split make plain-text transport and DDL rights visible and narrow; the platform cannot see whether a network between hosts is trusted.

### C250. Readiness, worker health and the internal listener (hardening 7.2; Phase 9 C175.2, C177, V13)

1. **`GET /ready`** (plugin-registered beside `/health`): Postgres `SELECT 1` and Valkey `PING`, each with a 1 s timeout; 200 `{status: 'ready'}` or 503 `{status: 'not_ready', checks: {postgres: 'ok' | 'unavailable', valkey: …}}` with no error text; never rate-limited; used for readiness only, never liveness, so a database blip does not restart every pod.
2. **Worker health:** the worker writes a heartbeat file (`OPENVERIFY_WORKER_HEARTBEAT_FILE`, default `/tmp/openverify-worker-heartbeat`) every 10 s from its event loop; `node dist/worker-health.js` exits 0 when the file is at most 30 s old, else 1, for an exec probe; no port is opened.
3. **`OPENVERIFY_INTERNAL_LISTEN`** (`host:port`): when set, the `/api/v1/internal/*` routes (Phase 9's flow-server embed-policy route and any later internal route) are registered only on a second Fastify listener at that address, and the public listener answers the generic 404 for them; when unset, today's behaviour stays (the flow-server token of Phase 9 C157.2 protects them) with a production warning. The chart sets it, routes the flow server to it, and also denies `/api/v1/internal/` at the ingress (C264.5). Tests: the route answers on the internal listener and not on the public one; the OpenAPI document stays without it.
4. **Shutdown:** the API drains in-flight requests on SIGTERM (as today); the worker stops taking jobs and finishes or releases its fenced claims (Phase 6 C74.1); the chart's `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` exceeds the longest job's lease (C263.4).
5. **The first organisation in production** (hardening 8.1's open question; `seed:dev` refuses production mode): `pnpm --filter @openverify/api org:create --name <name> --admin-email <email>` creates an organisation with both environments, the default template and `require-mfa: true` (Phase 9 C168.2), and sends a single-use invitation for its first admin by SMTP when the mailer is configured; it prints the link only on an interactive TTY with `--print-invitation` (review P22: a Kubernetes Job's or CI's stdout lands in a log store, where the link would let anyone claim the organisation), with a 24-hour lifetime by default; it creates no demo user and no API key, writes a system audit entry, and is idempotent by name (a second run refuses). `first-run.md` says never to run it as a logged Job with `--print-invitation`.
6. **Honesty:** readiness says the process can reach its stores now; it says nothing about the vision service or a provider, which have their own errors and alerts.
7. **Metrics as structured log events** (review H13; decision 72): the repository has no metrics system, and none is added: every metric the plan names (`rate_limiter_store_unavailable`, the in-flight, cap and admission-wait figures of C251.4, the export and peek refusals, the worker heartbeat's age) is a pino event with a stable `metric` field, a value and its labels (environment id, queue; never PII), registered in `apps/api/src/metrics-registry.ts` (`platform-ops`; the other 11B agents add their names as named borrowed edits). A test fails when the threat model, the guide's `monitoring.md` or the ASVS checklist names a metric that the registry does not hold, and `monitoring.md` documents log-based alerting on these events (a `/metrics` endpoint is deferred, C286).

### C251. Per-tenant queue quotas (hardening H1; connect K11; Phase 6 decision 22 after 6B; Phase 8 C117.8)

1. **One admission model** (decision 38): Phase 8's per-environment slots in Postgres (BullMQ's group fairness is commercial) are extended from workflow steps to every queue that holds one environment's work: verification processing, report runs (adverse media and the rest), Connect redemptions, account exports (C253), and workflow steps. A job is enqueued only while its environment has fewer than its cap of that queue's jobs queued or running; otherwise its row waits in its own outbox or `pending` state, and a fair sweep (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, environments taken in turn by `last_served_at`) admits it when a slot frees. **Admission is recorded** (review F12): verifications, report runs, share tokens and account exports gain `admitted_at` (the 11B migration), which the slot count reads; Phase 5's repair sweep (`jobs/repeatable/submitted-verifications.ts`) re-enqueues only an admitted verification left without a job, never one the cap holds back (a named borrowed edit by `quotas`, with a test).
2. **Caps:** default 50 per queue per environment, sandbox and production separately; the environment setting `queue-in-flight-caps` (`{<queue>: 1–ceiling}`, `settings:write`, audited) may lower a cap freely but never exceed the install ceiling `OPENVERIFY_QUEUE_IN_FLIGHT_CAP_MAX` (default 50, at most 1,000; review P15: a tenant cannot raise its own share of the worker on a multi-customer install); install-wide jobs (list syncs, the re-screen, retention, the face index rebuild) are not tenant work and keep their own single-flight rules.
3. **What a caller sees:** nothing changes on the API: a submitted verification stays `submitted` until processed, a report stays `pending`; the console's queue panel shows in-flight against cap per queue.
4. **Metrics and logs:** in-flight, cap and admission wait per environment and queue; a warn line when an environment sits at its cap for more than 5 minutes.
5. **Acceptance:** the load test's fairness scenario (C269.2): with one environment flooding report runs, another environment's report p95 stays within 2× its idle p95, at the default cap and with the flooder at the install ceiling (review P15); judged only by `calibration-load-11d`. 11B's own tests assert admission by counts and order (review H12).
6. **Honesty:** caps bound how much of the worker one tenant can hold; they do not add capacity, and a cap set too low delays that tenant's own work.

### C252. Operator-supplied patterns (hardening H16, G-REGEX, BX-D1; ASVS v5.0.0-15.2.2)

1. **Inventory first:** `contracts-11b` lists every place where operator or end-user text becomes a regular expression or a pattern (Phase 9's field `pattern`, Phase 7's list item patterns, Phase 8's condition operators, webhook and event filters, CSV import columns, any `new RegExp(` with a non-literal argument), with its `path:line`.
2. **One rule:** each goes through Phase 9's restricted grammar and linear-time matcher (C148.3, inputs capped at 10,000 characters) or is refused at write time; an ESLint rule (`no-restricted-syntax` on `new RegExp` with a non-literal first argument, and `RegExp(` calls) fails the lint outside an allowlist of reviewed call sites, each with its reason in the config.
3. **Tests:** a known catastrophic pattern (`(a+)+$` over 10,000 `a`s and a `!`) at every entry point, refused or matched within a 50 ms CPU budget.
4. **Closes** the Phase 0 carry ("RE2 or a time limit for operator-supplied regexes"), with a threat-model row (BX-D1) and an ASVS evidence path.
5. **Honesty:** a linear-time matcher bounds the cost of one match; the input caps and the rate limits bound the rest.

### C253. The DSAR access export (hardening H14; PLAN §3 "DSAR export", §9; decision 11)

1. **Route** (`POST /api/v1/accounts/:id/export`, new scope `accounts:export`, given to `admin` only by default; a user also needs `pii:view`): 202 with an `account-export` resource (`aexp_`, ours): `status` (`pending`, `ready`, `failed`, `expired`), `created-at`, `ready-at`, `expires-at`, `item-count`, `byte-count`; `GET /api/v1/account-exports/:id`; `GET /api/v1/account-exports/:id/manifest` (the JSON document) and `GET /api/v1/account-exports/:id/files/:file-id` (each file's bytes), both streamed, both audited. **Every export route** needs `accounts:export`, and a user also `pii:view` and Phase 9's 10-minute step-up (review P16), so a read-only key cannot download an export.
2. **The job** (queue `account-export`, under C251's cap): one JSON document (`application/json`, kebab-case, schema-versioned as `openverify.account-export.v1`) with the account and its fields; its inquiries with fields, statuses, sessions (time, consent records with their texts, IP and user agent), verifications with extracted data, check results and decisions; reports with their queries, matches and dispositions; device facts (Phase 7); transactions (Phase 7); Connect tokens and consent records on either side, with each recipient of a redeemed share (C241.4), the counterparty's rows read only through `cross-tenant.ts`'s `listSharesTouching` (C240.5); each file as a manifest entry with its kind, size and SHA-256. The manifest has two parts (review P16): `subject` (everything above) and `operator-review` (Phase 8's cases and their comments, Phase 7's list memberships and signals), labelled "review before disclosure", because some of it may fall under an exemption the operator must judge (for example a suspicion whose disclosure could tip off its subject); the compliance guide names those exemptions as questions for counsel (C278.4). Everything is read through the existing repositories with `pii:view` semantics. Only the manifest is stored, sealed (a new data key) in S3 under the export's id; **no file is copied**: the files route streams the live object and answers 410 `account_export_item_redacted` once it is redacted.
3. **Not covered, stated in the manifest and the guide:** API logs and webhook deliveries (kept 30 days by default and not indexed by account), event payloads (stubs after redaction), backups, and anything outside the install; face templates (derived numbers, useless outside the model) are listed as present with their count, not exported; whether that exclusion holds under the right of access is a question for counsel, listed under Q7 (review P16).
4. **Lifetime:** `account-export-ttl-days` (1–30, default 7); the retention pass deletes expired exports, and any redaction or retention deletion that touches an exported item (the account, an inquiry, a verification, a file, a report) deletes the exports that list it first.
5. **Limits:** one pending export per account (409 `account_export_pending`); at most 5,000 manifest items (422 `account_export_too_large`, with the counts, beyond it).
6. **Honesty:** the export is what this install holds about the account at that moment, through the account's links; an operator answering a request must still search for data not linked to the account and apply its own law's exemptions (the compliance guide, C278.4).
7. **Rate** (review P16): the policy `account-export`, 10 exports an hour per environment (provisional, ours), failing closed when Valkey is unreachable (503 `account_export_unavailable`), since one key exporting every account in turn is the platform's widest exfiltration path.

### C254. List ingestion carries: the UN opt-in and conditional requests (hardening H2, H3; connect K9, K10; Phase 6 decisions 10 and 21 after 6B)

1. **The UN Security Council list** (pending the owner, Q10): `contracts-11b` builds the switch and the acknowledgement, and they stay locked: an install setting `OPENVERIFY_WATCHLIST_UN_OPT_IN_ALLOWED` (default `false`, and `false` in every image and chart default) must be `true` before an environment admin can enable `un-sc` with an acknowledgement of the UN's terms (the text the sources document links, the user and time audited); while it is `false`, `un-sc` stays "cannot be enabled in this release" exactly as Phase 6 left it. 11G flips the documented default only if the owner confirms the reading of Q10.
2. **Conditional requests** (ours; decision 39): a source may opt in (`conditional: true` in `watchlists/sources.json`, only for publishers that send a strong `ETag` or `Last-Modified`); a check sends `If-None-Match` or `If-Modified-Since`; a 304 records the check as `validated-by: http-304` with its time and downloads nothing; a 200 is compared by SHA-256 as today. A full download and SHA-256 comparison still runs at least every `watchlist-full-check-hours` (1–168, default 24) whatever the 304s say, and the sources page shows both the last validation and the last full verification, so a publisher whose validator fails to move cannot keep a changed list fresh for more than that window (the honesty risk that dropped the change signal, decision 10 after 6B).
3. **Honesty:** a 304 is the publisher's statement that its representation is unchanged; the platform's own proof remains the periodic digest comparison.

### C255. Browser headers and request origin (G-HSTS, G-CSP, G-FRAME, B4-T1; ASVS v5.0.0-3.4.1, 3.4.3, 3.4.5, 3.4.6, 3.5)

1. **HSTS** (decision 40): the chart's ingress sets `Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains` for the API, flow and console hosts (no `preload` by default, a value the operator sets); for installs whose TLS terminator cannot add headers, `OPENVERIFY_HSTS_MAX_AGE` (0 = off, the default; the only HSTS variable, reviews P28, F25) makes the API, the flow server and the console send it; `platform-ops` builds all three, the flow server's and `apps/dashboard/next.config.ts`'s as named borrowed edits, each with a header test; the guide says which to use.
2. **The console's script CSP** (G-CSP) is Phase 9's (C170); Phase 11 verifies it: the ASVS row cites Phase 9's header tests, and `integration-11b` adds a test that every console route answers with the enforced policy and no `unsafe-inline` script source.
3. **The flow's response headers** (G-FRAME, H6) are Phase 9's flow server (C157); Phase 11 verifies them the same way, and the chart's ingress passes them through unchanged.
4. **Origin checks** (B4-T1, "to verify"): every state-changing console route handler and server action checks `Origin` (or `Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin` when `Origin` is absent) against the console's own origin; a test enumerates every handler under `apps/dashboard/src/app/**/route.ts` and every server action and fails on one without the check; the inventory records what Phase 9 built.
5. **Honesty:** headers instruct browsers; they do not protect an old browser or a client that ignores them.

### C256. Android in Phase 11 (Phase 10 C194.1, C213.4, decision 38; hardening B11-T1, B11-I1, B11-S1)

1. **The `share-consent` renderer** (C238.7), in `commonMain`, with host tests and a Roborazzi golden per state in `en` and `ar` (Phase 10 C219.3's budget).
2. **Certificate pinning** (B11-S1; decision 41): not built into the SDK, because each self-hoster has its own certificates and a pin set wrong locks every user out; the integration guide gives the recipe (an `OkHttpClient` with a `CertificatePinner` for the operator's API host, including a backup pin, installed through `OpenVerifyAndroidTransport.okHttp`, Phase 10 C191.1), and a host test proves a pinned client installed through the transport refuses a certificate outside its pins. **Owner** (review F24): `clients-connect` builds the host test at its Android checkpoint, over a TLS `MockWebServer` with certificates from `okhttp-tls`'s `HeldCertificate`, which `contracts-11a` adds in test scope with its `verification-metadata.xml` entry (C258.3); the recipe goes into Phase 10's `docs/android/` integration guide as a named borrowed edit.
3. **The threat-model rows** B11-T1 (rooted, emulated or hooked devices: accepted, the server never trusts the device), B11-I1 (data on the device: Phase 10 C213's controls as evidence) and B11-S1 (the pinning recipe and the host's network security configuration) are written in C273 with Phase 10's paths as evidence.
4. **MASVS v2.1.0** (C275) is fed by Phase 10's C213 and C219 evidence.
5. **Honesty:** pinning narrows which certificates a host app accepts; it does not help on a rooted device where the app itself can be modified.

### C257. Production images (hardening H7, 7.1, G-DEVIMG; ASVS v5.0.0-15.2.3; Phase 9 C175.2)

1. **Five images**, each multi-stage, non-root (a fixed non-zero uid), working with a read-only root filesystem and a writable `/tmp` only, production dependencies only, OCI labels (`org.opencontainers.image.source`, `.revision`, `.licenses`, `.version`), no `HEALTHCHECK` (the chart's probes decide):
   - `openverify/api` (`infra/docker/api.Dockerfile`): `apps/api` and its workspace packages compiled to `dist/` with `tsc -p tsconfig.build.json` (each package's build resets `customConditions`), pruned with `pnpm deploy --prod`; the commands live in `apps/api/scripts/`, outside `tsconfig.build.json`'s `rootDir: "src"`, so a second build config, `apps/api/tsconfig.commands.json` (`rootDir: "."`, emitting `dist/commands/`), compiles them (review F27); entry points `dist/server.js`, `dist/worker.js`, `dist/worker-health.js` and one compiled `dist/commands/<name>.js` per operator command (`db:migrate`, `db:grant-runtime`, `seed:dev`, `retention:run`, `kek:rewrap`, `blind-index:rebuild`, `face-index:rebuild`, `face-scan:benchmark`, `user:reset-mfa`, `user:allow-password`, `api-keys:pepper-status`, `watchlists:sync`, `watchlists:rekey`, `pep:sync`, `trust-anchors:sync`, `trust:sandbox-utopia`, and every command Phases 7–10 add, by the inventory); `package.json` keeps `tsx` for development, and a test fails when a script has no compiled counterpart. The data files the dev image bakes today (the watchlist variant groups and the three digests it re-checks) are baked the same way, each with its redistribution licence re-read by `contracts-11c` and recorded; a file whose terms do not allow redistribution is fetched at start instead.
   - `openverify/dashboard` (`infra/docker/dashboard.Dockerfile`): Next.js `output: 'standalone'`, `node server.js`, `NEXT_TELEMETRY_DISABLED=1`.
   - `openverify/flow` (`infra/docker/flow.Dockerfile`): Phase 9's flow server with the built bundle, YuNet (MIT, already served to every browser) and `onnxruntime-web`'s WASM; its publication follows Phase 4 decision 11 and Phase 10 P2 (Eigen inside `onnxruntime-web`), recorded in Q2, and its decline outcome is the same image built with `OPENVERIFY_FLOW_FACE_GUIDANCE=off` (C289). **Runtime configuration** (review F04; decision 73): the image's bundle is built without `VITE_OPENVERIFY_API_URL` (`apps/flow/vite.config.ts:23`, `src/main.tsx:36`). The flow server reads `OPENVERIFY_FLOW_API_URL` at start, serves it to the bundle as a same-origin `/config.json`, and sends `connect-src` with that origin as a response header, so one image digest serves any install's API host. **Both paths stay** (verification V07):
      - **Build-time path.** When the variable is set at build, which is what `vite`, `vite preview`, the per-push e2e (`e2e/setup/stack.ts:263, 307`) and the mocked flow suite (`apps/flow/e2e/playwright.config.ts:54`) do, `main.tsx` uses it as today, and the build-time `<meta>` keeps `connect-src` for it. That keeps Phase 9's defence in depth (`phase-9.plan.md:303`), and those runs stay green unchanged.
      - **Run-time path.** When the variable is unset at build (the image), `main.tsx` fetches `/config.json` before rendering, and the `<meta>` carries every directive except `connect-src`, which the server's header sets. Two policies intersect, so a `connect-src` in the meta would block the run-time origin.
      - **Edits.** No `stack.ts` or Vite dev-server edit is needed. A test builds both ways. `contracts-11c` freezes the variable; `images-11c` makes the change as named borrowed edits to Phase 9's flow server, `apps/flow/src/main.tsx`, `apps/flow/src/security/security-meta.ts` and `apps/flow/vite.config.ts`; `images.test.sh` runs one image against two API URLs without a rebuild.
   - `openverify/vision` (`services/vision/Dockerfile`): as today, with the model weights **not** in the image (decision 5): it reads `OPENVERIFY_MODELS_DIR` and refuses to start when a file's SHA-256 differs from `models/manifest.json`.
   - `openverify/code-runner` (Phase 8 P17's runner), with the `tcp:` transport of C263.6.
2. **Bases pinned by digest** (C258.1).
3. **The compose `app` profile** moves to these images; `infra/docker/apps.Dockerfile` is deleted once `infra/tests/apps-image-context.test.sh` covers the new Dockerfiles; `seed:dev` runs from the API image's compiled command and still refuses production mode. **The e2e stack keeps its host processes per push** (review F03; decision 74: `e2e/setup/stack.ts` runs the API, worker, vision, flow and dashboard on the host and the infrastructure in compose, and the CI `e2e` job builds no image) and gains an **images mode**: with `OPENVERIFY_E2E_IMAGES=1` the stack driver starts the five production images from a compose override (`e2e/setup/docker-compose.images.yml`, written by `images-11c`) instead of the host processes, through a driver branch owned by `integration-11c`. The images mode runs locally at 11C's checkpoints, for DAST (C283), never per push (the load stack is the compose `app` profile with its own override, C268.4; verification V10); its duration is measured and recorded.
4. **Tests** (`infra/tests/images.test.sh`, extending the existing image-context test): each image builds; runs as its uid with `--read-only --tmpfs /tmp`; contains no `tsx`, `vitest`, `typescript`, `@playwright/*`, `eslint` or `vite` (`find` over `node_modules` against a denylist); answers its own smoke (`/health` for the API, the static index and the CSP header for the flow, `/login` for the dashboard, `/health` with models mounted for vision); and `docker image inspect` shows the labels.
5. **Published images redistribute the base distribution's packages**, some under the GPL (ordinary aggregation; not legal advice); the release notes point at the distribution's source archive for each base digest (hardening 7.1), and the image SBOM lists them (C261).
6. **Honesty:** the images are what the release ships; nothing is published until Q2.

### C258. Pins: digests, checksums and the release-age record (hardening 5.2, 10.9; decision 42)

1. **Images by digest:** every `FROM` in every Dockerfile, every `image:` in `infra/docker-compose.yml` and the e2e compose file, and every image the chart names carries `tag@sha256:…`; the Testcontainers images keep the tags `CLAUDE.md` names and add their digests (`pgvector/pgvector:pg17@sha256:…`, `valkey/valkey:9.1@sha256:…`, `rustfs/rustfs:1.0.3@sha256:…`); a test fails on an unpinned reference. `infra/scripts/refresh-digests.sh` resolves a tag to its digest and records the date, run by hand only.
2. **Binaries by checksum:** every scanner and tool that ships a release binary is installed by an `infra/scripts/install-<tool>.sh` in the gitleaks pattern (version, SHA-256 pinned in the repository and checked against the release's checksum file), and its signature is verified with cosign (itself checked by SHA-256 only) where the project publishes one. pip-audit publishes no binary, so it runs from a uv tool project of its own, `security/pip-audit/`, with a hashed lockfile whose every transitive licence is recorded (review F09). No third-party scanner action is used, ever (the Trivy compromise of 2026-03-19, CVE-2026-33634: 76 of 77 `trivy-action` tags force-pushed; images referenced by digest were unaffected).
3. **The pins** (each released on or before 2026-10-15 and re-read by the adopting Stage 1a on its own day; a newer release is taken only if it is at least 14 days old then, recorded with its date):

   | Tool | Version (date) | Licence | Use |
   |---|---|---|---|
   | Trivy | v0.76.0 (2026-10-14) | Apache-2.0 | `config` scans per push; `image` scans in `release.yml`; its checks bundle pinned by digest |
   | osv-scanner | v2.7.0 (2026-10-12) | Apache-2.0 | lockfiles (`pnpm-lock.yaml`, `uv.lock`, `gradle.lockfile`, `verification-metadata.xml`) |
   | Grype | v0.120.1 (2026-10-06) | Apache-2.0 | images and SBOMs in `release.yml` |
   | Syft | v1.54.1 (2026-10-06) | Apache-2.0 | SBOMs of images and source trees |
   | zizmor | v1.31.1 (2026-10-15T23:16Z; v1.31.0 if an adopting agent counts the 14 days to the minute before 2026-10-29T23:16Z) | MIT | workflow audits |
   | pip-audit | 2.10.1 (2026-06-10), from `security/pip-audit/`'s lockfile | Apache-2.0 | vision and `load/` environments |
   | gitleaks | 8.30.1 (in use) | MIT | secrets, and the PII-pattern rules |
   | ZAP | v2.17.0 (2025-12-15), image by digest | Apache-2.0 | DAST (C283) |
   | cosign | v3.1.3 (2026-08-06) | Apache-2.0 | signature checks of tools now; signing at release (Q3) |
   | Helm | v4.3.1 and v3.22.1 (2026-10-15) | Apache-2.0 | chart lint, template, install |
   | helm-unittest | v1.2.1 (2026-10-03) | MIT | chart unit tests |
   | kubeconform | v0.8.0 (2026-06-04) | Apache-2.0 | manifest schema checks, offline schemas for 1.35–1.37 |
   | chart-testing (`ct`) | v3.15.0 (2026-10-06) | Apache-2.0 | `ct install` only (review F13: `ct lint` runs yamllint, GPL-3.0, which fails rule 4 even as tooling; lint is `helm lint` and kubeconform) |
   | kind and its node images | the newest kind release dated on or before 2026-10-15 and its 1.35 and 1.37 node images by digest, read by `contracts-11c` (version, date, licence file, checksum) | Apache-2.0 | the kind clusters of C265.4 |
   | kubectl | the newest 1.35 patch release dated on or before 2026-10-15, by checksum, read by `contracts-11c` | Apache-2.0 | `ct install` and `kind.sh` |
   | Kubernetes JSON schemas for kubeconform | generated from each release's OpenAPI document (1.35, 1.36, 1.37, by tag and SHA-256) by `scripts/fetch_k8s_schemas.py` with kubeconform's own `openapi2jsonschema` step, never from a third-party schema repository (whose archives are not byte-stable), read by `contracts-11c` | Apache-2.0 (Kubernetes) | C265.3 |
   | cdxgen | 12.8.6, only if C261.1's fallback is taken, its date and licence read by `contracts-11c` | Apache-2.0 | pnpm workspace SBOMs |
   | `actions/attest-build-provenance` | v4.2.2 by commit, its date read by `contracts-11c` | MIT | provenance, 11G only (Q1, Q3) |
   | `slsa-github-generator` | v2.1.0 by commit, its date read by `contracts-11c` | Apache-2.0 | the alternative provenance path, 11G only |
   | `okhttp-tls` (test scope) | the version matching Phase 10's pinned OkHttp, at least 14 days old, read by `contracts-11a` | Apache-2.0 | C256.2's host test (`HeldCertificate`) |
   | Locust | 2.46.7 (2026-10-04), through uv, **only if Q12 is granted** (its ZPL-2.1 and MPL-2.0 dependencies) | MIT | multi-step load scenarios |
   | httpx | the newest release dated on or before 2026-10-15, through uv, read by `contracts-11d` (its dependency tree's licences recorded) | BSD-3-Clause | the `load/driver/` |
   | vegeta | v12.13.0 (2025-10-31) | MIT | constant-rate route latency |
   | eslint-plugin-security | 4.2.0 (2026-10-01), through pnpm | Apache-2.0 | SAST rules |
   | CycloneDX Gradle plugin | 3.5.1 (2026-10-09), through `pluginManagement` | Apache-2.0 | Android SBOMs |
   | `openid-client` with `oauth4webapi` and `jose` | 6.8.8, 3.8.8, ^6.2.12 (2026-09-05) | MIT | C248 |

4. **The release-age rule for toolchain binaries, base images and actions** (Q5): every pin this plan adds follows the 14 days, which complies whichever way the owner reads the rule; whether the rule itself covers binaries, images and actions, and what happens to uv 0.13.3 (pinned in CI and the vision Dockerfile the day it was released, 2026-10-20), is the owner's (Q5), and 11G applies the answer.
5. **Honesty:** a pin and a checksum prove the bytes are the ones reviewed; they do not prove that the reviewed release was benign.

### C259. Dependency, workflow and configuration scanning (hardening 5.1, 5.3; G-SBOM V15.1.1; decisions 6, 43)

1. **The `supply-chain` job** (per push, one job so its minutes round once; C267.2):
   - osv-scanner over `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `services/vision/uv.lock`, `load/uv.lock`, every `android/**/gradle.lockfile` and `android/gradle/verification-metadata.xml` (Phase 10 C214.5);
   - `pnpm audit --audit-level high` and `pnpm audit signatures` (the registry's ECDSA signatures against the lockfile's integrity);
   - pip-audit over `uv export --frozen --format requirements-txt` for vision and `load/`, with `--require-hashes --disable-pip --no-deps`;
   - zizmor over `.github/workflows/` and `.github/actions/`, failing at medium or above (`unpinned-uses`, `unpinned-images`, `impostor-commit`, `ref-version-mismatch`, `excessive-permissions`, `template-injection`, `artipacked` among its audits), beside actionlint (in use);
   - Trivy `config` over `infra/` and the rendered chart, failing at high;
   - the chart's lint, unit tests and schema checks (C265.1–C265.3);
   - the PII-pattern scan (C260.3).
2. **The gate** (decision 6; review H14): every finding at critical or high, with or without a fix, fails the job unless `security/allowlist.yaml` holds an entry `{id, package or path, reason, expires (at most 90 days ahead), added-by}` (the reason may be "no fix available"); an expired entry fails the job; the same rule gates the image scans in `release.yml`; medium and low are reported in the step summary, and `pnpm release:check` lists every medium whose advisory was published longer ago than its 90-day time frame. Remediation time frames (ASVS v5.0.0-15.1.1): critical 7 days, high 30, medium 90, low the next release, written in `docs/security/vulnerability-policy.md` (C276.4).
3. **Network:** the scanners query OSV, the npm registry and PyPI at run time (read-only, no credentials); each vulnerability database is fetched fresh per run, or refused when its build date is older than 24 hours, and its build date is written in the step summary (review H14: a cached database would report a weeks-old view as clean; Trivy's checks bundle, which is policy and not vulnerability data, stays pinned by digest); nothing secret is in the job's environment (`permissions: contents: read`, `persist-credentials: false`).
4. **Locally** (Validation): the same script (`infra/scripts/supply-chain.sh`) runs every check above, so a failure is reproduced before a push.
5. **Honesty:** a scanner finds known, published weaknesses in what it can see; a clean scan is not a security review.

### C260. SAST and the PII-pattern scan (hardening 5.3, H15; PLAN §9; decisions 2, 16)

1. **SAST baseline** (decision 2; Semgrep's engine and Opengrep are LGPL-2.1 and Semgrep's rules are source-available, so all fail rule 4; CodeQL's terms allow only OSI-licensed codebases and code scanning on a private repository needs a paid licence, Q9): ESLint with `eslint-plugin-security` 4.2.0, configured in `packages/config/eslint/security.js` and wired into the root `eslint.config.js` (there is no preset: the root file imports `packages/config/eslint/*.js` today; `contracts-11c` adds the plugin's dependency to the root `package.json` in Stage 1a, and the root `eslint.config.js` line that loads `security.js` is `sast-pii-11c`'s named borrowed edit; review F28 and its verification), each rule on or off with its reason in the config (on: `detect-unsafe-regex`, `detect-eval-with-expression`, `detect-child-process`, `detect-non-literal-require`, `detect-buffer-noassert`, `detect-new-buffer`, `detect-pseudoRandomBytes`, `detect-possible-timing-attacks`, `detect-bidi-characters`, `detect-disable-mustache-escape`, `detect-no-csrf-before-method-override`; off with a reason: `detect-object-injection`, which flags every keyed access in typed code; `detect-non-literal-regexp`, because C252.2's custom rule with its reviewed allowlist covers the same calls; `detect-non-literal-fs-filename`, on for `apps/api/scripts/`, `scripts/` and `security/` and off elsewhere with the reason that the services never take a path from input, a source test asserting it); ruff's `S` rules (in use); Android lint with its security checks (in use). Existing findings are fixed, or suppressed inline with a reason, and a test counts suppressions per rule against a committed ceiling.
2. **Not adopted:** `eslint-plugin-no-unsanitized` (MPL-2.0; React escapes and no code writes `innerHTML`, which C252's rule and a source scan keep so); CodeQL until Q1 or Q9 allows it.
3. **The PII-pattern scan** (decision 16): a second gitleaks configuration, `security/gitleaks-pii.toml`, over `contracts/fixtures/`, `fixtures/`, `docs/`, `e2e/`, every `test-support/` and every test file, with rules for email addresses outside `example.com`, `example.org` and `example.net`; IPv4 outside the documentation ranges (192.0.2.0/24, 198.51.100.0/24, 203.0.113.0/24), loopback, `10.0.2.2` and `0.0.0.0`; IPv6 outside `2001:db8::/32` and `::1`; phone numbers outside the fictitious ranges the fixtures use; and MRZ-shaped lines whose name fields are not in the synthetic generator's name list; Luhn-valid card numbers outside the networks' documented test ranges, checksum-valid IBANs outside the published examples, and US SSN-shaped values outside the never-issued ranges (review P29). The scanned paths also include the seed sources (`seed:dev`, `seed:dev --connect`, `seed:load`) and `load/`, which create the synthetic people; `sast-pii-11c` runs the new configuration once over the full history and records the result in its TDD file. A reviewed allowlist (`security/gitleaks-pii-allowlist.toml`, each entry with a reason) covers the vendor's public example values the research notes quote by id prefix only, and the pre-commit hook runs it on staged files, failing closed.
4. **Honesty:** pattern rules catch what looks like personal data; they cannot tell a real name from a synthetic one, so synthetic generators remain the rule (rule 3).

### C261. SBOMs (hardening 5.4, G-SBOM V15.1.2; decision 3)

1. **What gets one:** each image (Syft over the built image, CycloneDX JSON), the source trees (Syft over `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `services/vision/uv.lock`, `load/uv.lock`; cdxgen 12.8.6 is the fallback only if `contracts-11c`'s spike shows Syft's pnpm cataloguer misses workspace packages, adding its lockfile entries with licence rows), every published Android AAR (the CycloneDX Gradle plugin's `cyclonedxBom` per module, Phase 10's artifacts; `contracts-11c` adds the plugin with its verification metadata in Stage 1a under the Gradle lock, after a spike on `:openverify-sdk`'s Kotlin Multiplatform module, and if the plugin cannot read it the fallback is Syft over the published AAR's POM and the Gradle lockfiles; review F29), and each npm package (Syft over its packed tarball).
2. **A licence check over the SBOMs** (`security/check-sbom-licences.py`, `python3 -I`): every component of a runtime image or a published package has a licence in rule 4's list, a §5 tooling exception that never ships (which then fails it, because it shipped), or an entry in `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md` with a recorded decision; unknown licences fail. A shipped component whose entry still reads "owner to confirm" or "pending owner decision" never passes silently (review H19: `nodemailer`'s MIT-0 until Q11 is answered): the check reports it as `owner`, the staged artifact is labelled `pending-owner` in its name and in the release notes, and `pnpm release:check --tag` (the owner's tag step, C284.2) refuses the release while any remains without a decision or a built decline outcome; staging and the default `pnpm release:check` of every validation stay green, reporting it, so 11A–11F do not wait on the owner (verification V01). The check also reads the hand-listed vendored and compiled-in components of `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md` per artifact (review H20: Eigen inside `onnxruntime-web`, which Syft catalogues as MIT) under the same rule, and the flow image's decline outcome of C289's P2 row is staged beside it. The base distribution's packages are checked against a separate list (aggregation, C257.5).
3. **While the repository is private** (decision 3): SBOMs are built in `release.yml` and in Validation, kept as workflow artifacts and attached to the release only when it is published (Q1, Q2).
4. **Honesty:** an SBOM lists what the tool resolved; vendored or generated code it cannot see is listed by hand in `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md`, as today.

### C262. Signing and provenance (owner-gated: Q1, Q2, Q3; hardening 5.4)

1. **What is built now:** `release.yml`'s publish job with cosign v3.1.3 signing of each image digest and its SBOM as an attestation and SLSA v1.2 Build-track provenance (GitHub artifact attestations with `actions/attest-build-provenance` v4.2.2 on a public repository; `slsa-github-generator` v2.1.0's reusable workflow as the alternative; both in C258.3's pin table). The `cosign verify` commands with the expected identity enter the self-hosting guide only when 11G wires the publish job (C277.1, review H16).
2. **Behind a switch:** the job runs only in the GitHub environment `release` (deployment branch `main`, a required reviewer where the plan offers one) and only when the repository variable `OPENVERIFY_PUBLISH` is `true`; until then `release.yml` stages artifacts and exits with a notice (C267.1).
3. **Why it waits:** keyless signing publishes the workflow's repository and identity to Sigstore's public transparency log, which exposes a private repository's name; artifact attestations on a private repository need GitHub Enterprise Cloud; environments on a private repository need a paid plan. Each is the owner's (Q1, Q3).
4. **If the owner declines:** nothing is signed or attested; the release notes give each artifact's SHA-256, and operators build from source with the documented Dockerfiles.
5. **Honesty:** a signature proves which workflow built an artifact; it does not prove the source was free of defects.

### C263. The Helm chart: structure and values (hardening 7.2, 7.4; decisions 12, 44)

1. **Path and metadata:** `deploy/helm/openverify/` with `Chart.yaml` (`apiVersion: v2`, `kubeVersion: ">=1.35.0-0"`, `version` and `appVersion` from the release), `values.yaml` (every default commented, no secret value), `values.schema.json` (validated by Helm), `templates/` per component (`api/`, `worker/`, `vision/`, `flow/`, `dashboard/`, `code-runner/`, `migrate-job.yaml`, `networkpolicies.yaml`, `ingress.yaml` with an optional Gateway API `HTTPRoute`, `configmap.yaml`, `_helpers.tpl`), `tests/` (helm-unittest), `ci/` (values for `ct install`). No subchart and no Bitnami dependency (their Debian images moved to a legacy registry).
2. **Values** (hardening 7.2's shape): `image.{registry, repository, tag, digest}` per component, the digest preferred; `imagePullSecrets`; `external.postgres.{existingSecret, key}` and `external.postgres.migrationSecret` (C249.3); `external.valkey.existingSecret`; `external.s3.{endpoint, region, bucket, forcePathStyle, existingSecret}`; `external.smtp.existingSecret`; `secrets.<process>.existingSecret`, one per process holding only what it reads (C264.3); `api.trustProxy`, `dashboard.trustedProxyHops`; `ingress.{className, hosts, tls, hsts, maxBodySize}`; per component `replicaCount`, `resources`, `autoscaling`, `podDisruptionBudget`, `topologySpreadConstraints`, `priorityClassName`; `networkPolicy.{enabled, egress.{webhooks, providers, listSyncs, modelHosts, dataStores, smtp, identityProviders}}`, where `dataStores`, `smtp` and `identityProviders` are lists of CIDR and port, required by the schema when a store is external and policies are on (review P19: managed Postgres, Valkey, S3 and SMTP in a VPC, and an on-premises identity provider, sit on private addresses the internet rule excludes); `models.{source: job | pvc, existingClaim, storageClass, accessMode}` (review P05). **No `flow.allowedFrameAncestors`:** the flow server reads each environment's allowlist per request (Phase 9 C175.2).
3. **The migrate job:** a `pre-install,pre-upgrade` hook with `hook-delete-policy: before-hook-creation,hook-succeeded`, `backoffLimit: 1`, `activeDeadlineSeconds` above the lock timeout, running `dist/commands/db-migrate.js` with the migration secret; the advisory lock of C249.4 serialises a retried hook or a GitOps double apply; a values switch renders it as a plain, versioned Job for GitOps tools that do not run hooks.
